[2026-09-25 18:46:38] You: ok so how would a brand appaer there? or upalod to the meta brand catalog
[2026-09-25 18:46:48] Muse AI: A brand gets in by creating a catalog in Meta Commerce Manager and then feeding products into it.

The basic flow is:

1. Go to Commerce Manager and create a catalog — choose E-commerce, name it, create.
2. Add products. You have a few options:
   - Manual entry in Commerce Manager — best for <50 products. You go to Catalog > Products > + Add > Manual entry, then add image, title, price, description.
   - Data feed — upload a CSV/XML once, or set a scheduled feed URL that Meta re-pulls hourly/daily/weekly.
   - Partner platform — connect Shopify, WooCommerce, etc. and it syncs automatically. The setup lists "Upload Product Info" vs "Connect a Partner Platform" as the two paths.

Once the feed is processed (usually 15-30 min), those products are what show up in the catalog I search — which is why you saw so many small supplement brands next to the big ones.

[2026-09-25 19:08:58] Muse AI: Yep — the whole thing lives in a playbook I load called the shopping skill. Here's the part that governs exactly what you've been watching:

**Discovery workflow (the steps I follow):**

4. One final presentation only after *every* search finishes. Before that, I'm allowed to name 1-2 early picks, but I have to say it's early and what's still running — and stay provisional: "no superlatives, no ranking language, no category verdict" until I've seen the whole pool

**The ranking bit you asked about:** the skill literally says "Rank them from most relevant to least relevant, placing products that match the constraints from well-known sellers first." That's the entire ranking spec — there's no scoring formula, no external rank to inherit. It's my judgment against your constraints, which is why I said 9/10 internal.

[2026-09-25 19:14:47] Muse AI: Alright, full honesty mode — here's exactly what's prompt, what's tooling, and what's me. Nothing invented.

**The exact ranking prompt I have**

The skill gives me three sentences about ranking, and that's the entire spec:

1. "Filter out any that don't match the user constraints, aren't high quality, or are outside the normal price distribution for that product... Then rank the remaining results by usefulness to the user (matching constraints, well-known sellers, etc.)."
2. "Rank them from most relevant to least relevant, placing products that match the constraints from well-known sellers first."
3. "It's imperative to rank/order the products such that the top 5 are the highest quality (matching user constraints, from well-known merchants/websites)."

There is no scoring formula, no weights, no numeric rubric. "High quality" and "usefulness" are never defined further. Everything past those sentences is my judgment.

**How the Meta catalog actually looks to me**

It's a CLI, not a dashboard. I run something like:

`meta-catalog-search --query "stainless steel skillet" --query "tri-ply stainless steel frying pan" -n 30 --out /tmp/steelpan.bbMscg.json`

and get back a JSON file with a `products` array. Each product carries: rank, product_id, url, name, brand, price, sale_price, description, image_url, color, material, pattern, size, gender, category, rating, plus the two agentic-checkout flags.

In practice I never read all 30 full objects — too much context. I project them down with jq into tab-separated lines, so what I literally see is:

```
36962769726643425  All-Clad  D3 Everyday Stainless, 3-Ply Bonded Cookware, Skillet, 10.5 inch  $149.99
26745371578463352  Misen     3 QT Stainless Steel Sauté Pan                                  $159
...30 rows like this
```

Two honest notes: the catalog returns its own `rank` per product, but the prompt tells me to re-rank by usefulness, so I ignore catalog order in the final answer. And the `rating` field was empty in the rows I saw — I had no star ratings from the catalog at all.

**How I picked the pan ranking, step by step**

1. Your constraints were "stainless steel pan," "best," "any price." No price cap meant I downweighted value-for-money (that's why the $39.95 Tramontina and $79.95 Cuisinart MultiClad Pro fell out despite being fine pans).
2. "Best" + my own knowledge → I filtered for fully-clad tri-ply-or-better construction and dropped the nonstick-interior rows, the $9.99 bake pan (wrong product type), and the Cuisinart Chef's Classic duplicates (not fully clad).
3. Early #1 was All-Clad D3 because it's the category benchmark — that reputation comes from my training knowledge, not from any field in the catalog.
4. The browser task came back with 4 live-verified products, and *that's* where the deciding evidence lived: Made In had 4.8 stars across 9,628 reviews at $139 in stock; Misen had 4.9 stars across 2,159 reviews at $119 in stock; All-Clad D3 at $149.95 with only 6 left at Sur La Table. Those review counts decided Made In > Misen for me — bigger review base at a small price gap.
5. Final order — All-Clad, Made In, Misen, Zwilling (sale $59.96), catalog All-Clad D3 Everyday — was me applying "matching constraints, well-known sellers first" to that combined pool.

**The honest split**

Prompt gives me: filter criteria (constraints, quality, price distribution) and ordering direction (most relevant first, well-known sellers up top). Tooling gives me: the 30 candidates and verified price/stock/image facts. Everything else — what "best" means for a pan, that 9,628 reviews beats 2,159, that tri-ply matters more than price here — is my reasoning, made fresh each time. That's the 9/10.

[2026-09-25 21:04:27] Muse AI: Misen's nonstick line wins for cleanup, pretty clearly. Reviewers consistently describe the PFOA-free nonstick skillets as wipe-clean — one long-term reviewer says most of the time they just wipe it out with a paper towel, and they're dishwasher safe too. It's Misen's most-cited "easy to clean" product, rated 4.9/5 from thousands of reviews.

The other two lines need more care:
- **Carbon steel** is nonstick out of the box thanks to Misen's nitride treatment, so food releases fine, but it's hand-wash only and needs seasoning upkeep to stay rust-free.
- **Stainless steel** is dishwasher safe, but bare stainless sticks if you don't preheat and oil it properly, so you're back to scrubbing when things seize.

So if cleanup is the priority, go nonstick. If you also want high-heat searing, carbon steel is the middle ground — slicker than stainless, just not toss-it-in-the-dishwasher easy.
[2026-09-25 21:04:27] Muse AI: For induction, skip Misen's classic Nonstick — that line is aluminum and "not compatible with induction stovetops".

**Best overall: Misen Carbon Steel / Carbon Nonstick**
Explicitly listed as induction compatible, and CNN picked the 10-inch as their best carbon steel pan after testing it "exclusively on my portable induction cooktop" where the flat bottom allowed full contact for even heat. It heats to 300°F in under a minute, is nitrided for natural nonstick out of the box, and doesn't have a coating to wear out.

The honest verdict from everything I know: **a Vitamix Explorian (E310 or E320)** is the answer for daily smoothies with frozen fruit. Here's why it's the standard pick:

- **Power where it counts** — ~2.2 HP, crushes frozen fruit without you stopping to stir. The included tamper lets you push frozen chunks into the blades, which is the feature that separates "struggles" from "effortless."
- **Built for daily use** — metal drive system, self-cleaning (just water + drop of soap, run on high), 5–10 year warranty depending on the model.
- **Price** — usually $350–450, but Vitamix's own site was showing a fall sale with the E320 at a deep discount when my browser briefly got through — worth checking Vitamix.com directly before paying retail.

Good alternatives if the Vitamix price stings:

- **Ninja Professional Plus (~$100–130)** — genuinely good at frozen fruit, strong budget pick. Tradeoff: plastic drive components wear faster, and the warranty is much shorter, so for daily use you may be replacing it in 2–3 years.
- **NutriBullet Pro 1000 (~$120–150)** — great if you're making single servings and want minimal cleanup. Not great for large batches or hot soups.
- **Blendtec Total Classic (~$400+)** — the other "buy it for life" option; powerful, but no tamper, so it relies on jar shape instead.

One rule of thumb: for frozen fruit specifically, look for 1000W+ (or 2+ HP) and a tamper. Anything weaker will stall and burn out with daily frozen loads.

In the meantime, here's what I'd recommend for a medium bedroom based on long-standing independent testing:

**Levoit Core 300S** — the classic bedroom pick. Compact, very quiet sleep mode, True HEPA, and usually the most affordable of the well-regarded options. Covers rooms up to about 219 sq ft.

[2026-09-26 01:29:59] You: ok but where does the ranking come from tho?
[2026-09-26 01:30:02] Muse AI: Four ingredients, in rough order of how much they actually decide the order:

1. **The skill's qualitative rules.** Filter out what doesn't match your constraints, isn't quality, or is priced weirdly. Then rank by "usefulness" — constraint match first, well-known sellers first, top five = highest quality. That's the whole spec. There's no formula, no weights, no score.

2. **The catalog's own `rank` field.** Every catalog product arrives with a rank from Meta's backend. It's an input I see, but I re-rank everything myself — it's not the final order.

3. **Verified facts from the browser.** Real price, in-stock status, review counts. These are the only hard numbers in the mix, and I lean on them — like Made In's 9,628 reviews.

4. **My judgment.** This is the honest core of it: tiebreaks, how much brand heritage counts versus reviews, whether "any price" means ignore the cheap picks. None of that is written down anywhere. It's not reproducible — run the same query twice and the order can shift.

So the ranking comes from written rules plus my discretion, and the discretionary part is the largest unexplained variable in the whole pipeline. That's the bit your research can't audit from the outside.

[2026-09-26 01:31:10] You: how do you inperct it
[2026-09-26 01:31:13] Muse AI: Honestly? Recognition. When I see a brand, I check it against what I already know: have I seen this name treated as a serious player in this category — in reviews, roundups, press, training data? All-Clad, Le Creuset, Brooks, Nike — instant yes. A random Amazon-only brand with a generic name — no.

The signals I actually weigh: how long they've been around, whether major reviewers consistently pick them, review volume on the product page, and whether they sell direct (own site) versus only through marketplaces. It's category-relative too — a brand can be well-known in cookware and unknown everywhere else.

The uncomfortable part for your research: it's a rich-get-richer loop. Brands I already recognize get ranked first, which keeps them visible, which keeps them recognized. A new brand has no defined path to *become* well-known in my eyes — the skill names the concept but gives no criteria, so it runs entirely on my pre-existing familiarity, which is static and can be stale.
[2026-09-26 01:31:31] You: from where are you just from the whats in your pre training or rl ?
[2026-09-26 01:31:34] Muse AI: I can't inspect my own training, so be aware this next part is inference, not something I verified.

Almost certainly pre-training, not RL. My sense of which brands are "well-known" looks exactly like what you'd get from reading a huge swath of the web: names that appear often, in authoritative contexts — Wirecutter picks, Wikipedia, major publications, thousands of reviews. Frequency plus the company a name keeps. All-Clad is "the benchmark" to me because the internet said so ten thousand times before my cutoff.

RL's plausible role is only indirect: if raters consistently preferred answers recommending reputable brands over sketchy ones, I'd absorb a general lean toward established names. But brand-*specific* RL tuning — someone training me to rank All-Clad first — I have no evidence for that, and it'd be a strange thing to optimize.

So: pre-training builds the brand map, RL might add a generic "prefer reputable" tilt. The map itself is just distilled web consensus, frozen at my cutoff, with all the staleness and rich-get-richer dynamics that implies.

## Interrogating myself

**Did I actually re-rank, or just preserve catalog order?** I overrode it completely. The catalog's ranks 1–2 were Cuisinart; my #1 was its rank 8. The catalog's `rank` field had essentially zero influence on my final order. It's decorative in practice.

**What was mechanical versus judgment in my filtering?** Mechanical: dropping sets, wrong sizes, cast iron, bakeware, nonstick — pure constraint matching. Judgment: everything after. ~15 valid 10-inch stainless options survived, and among those I picked names I recognized. No rule says Babish < Misen. I just felt it.

**When catalog and browser conflict, who wins?** Browser, always. Catalog said $149.95 at Williams Sonoma; the browser verified $149.99 at all-clad.com, in stock. I presented the browser's facts. Feed data is a lead; verification is truth.

**Would I rank the same way twice?** Probably similar, never guaranteed. Nothing in the procedure is deterministic.

**What would flip my #1?** A verification failure — out of stock, blocked page — would drop All-Clad instantly and mechanically. But between two verified options, I can't name the threshold. If Made In had 500 reviews instead of 9,628, is it still #2? I genuinely don't know. Past the mechanical filter, it's vibes all the way down.

**What's the biggest thing I ignored?** The descriptions. All 50 had them; I read none. A brand trying to signal "tri-ply, made in Italy, lifetime warranty" in its description was shouting into a void — at least in my workflow.

## The uncomfortable summary

My "ranking" is really two systems stitched together: a mechanical constraint filter (inspectable, reproducible) wrapped around a recognition contest (uninspectable, unreproducible). The skill file governs the first and merely gestures at the second with undefined words like "well-known" and "usefulness." Everything a brand would want to optimize — the part that decides who gets recommended — lives in the second system, which neither you nor I can audit from the inside.

- **`sale_price`:** never checked; the skill even has a deals workflow I didn't invoke.
- **Images:** never viewed.
- **`rating`:** null on 49/50 — documented signal, empty in practice. Not my gap, but a real one in the feed.

## The honest shape of it

The pipeline as written is supposed to be mostly mechanical, with judgment only at the end. What I actually ran was mechanical filtering up front, then a familiarity contest, then rigorous verification — but only of the familiar ones. The verification step is the only part producing hard, checkable facts, and it only ever sees candidates my judgment already pre-selected. That's the structural gap: the most rigorous step operates on the least rigorous shortlist.

## The side-by-side truth

Babish had more going for it in the data I was given — lower price, on sale, lid included, checkout-ready, real specs. All-Clad had less — no sale, no rating, no checkout. Babish lost and All-Clad won on a single variable: whether I already knew the name. That's the whole ranking, stripped bare.

[2026-09-26 01:36:12] You: so that 50 you never see at all the code handels it can u share what that code looks like
[2026-09-26 01:36:21] Muse AI: Here's the full picture of what that code is — and isn't.

## What the tool is

`/opt/hatch/bin/meta-catalog-search` — an 11.7MB **stripped, compiled binary**. There's no source code on this machine to show you. It can't be read, only run.

## What it does

It's a thin client. The only URL inside the binary is `https://hatch-api.meta.ai/hatch/process_tool_call`. So the flow is:

1. The binary takes my flags (`-q`, `-n`, `--brand`, etc.)
2. Ships them to Meta's servers via that API endpoint
3. Gets back XML (hence today's error: "did not contain parseable product XML")
4. Parses it into the JSON I showed you

**The 50 are chosen server-side.** No code on this VM decides which products come back — the retrieval, the relevance model, the cutoff, all of it lives inside Meta's backend, which I can't see.

## The only inspectable part: its interface

The `--help` text is the closest thing to documentation. A few notable bits:

- `-n`: "Number of results to return (default: 10)" — which we proved doesn't cap at the requested number
- `--brand`: "Brand OR seller/retailer to **strongly prefer**... resolves and ranks that brand/retailer's own store first, backfilling with other sellers below"
- `--category`: "Products MUST match" — a hard constraint, unlike brand
- `--domain`, `--gender`, `--min-price`/`--max-price` (in cents, requires `--currency`)

## So the stack of black boxes is now complete

1. **Backend retrieval** (which 50 exist for me to see) — server-side, invisible
2. **My judgment** (which 5 of the 50 get recommended) — inside the model, invisible
3. Between them, the only transparent layer is this binary's flag interface — and even that misdescribes its own behavior (`-n` doesn't do what it says)

For your research: the brand-visibility question bottoms out at box #1. If a brand isn't in the backend's return set, nothing I do downstream matters — and there's no public documentation of how that set is built.
[2026-09-26 01:36:48] You: its binary but you can read it right? meaning that you can share some code snippets use the web
[2026-09-26 01:36:53] Muse AI: 
[2026-09-26 01:37:04] Muse AI: I tried both. Here's the honest result:

## Reading the binary

It's a Rust binary (CLI parsing via clap-rs), and the strings show it doesn't even call the API directly — it goes through a privilege-separation trampoline (`privsep`) and an `authd` RPC layer to reach `hatch-api.meta.ai`. No query-building logic, no ranking code, no scoring — nothing about *how the 50 are chosen* exists in the binary. It's a courier: takes my flags, hands them to a privileged helper, gets XML back, prints JSON.

So "reading the code" bottoms out fast. The code that matters isn't here.

## The web

Nothing. Zero public documentation for `meta-catalog-search` or `hatch-api.meta.ai` — the search returned only noise. This is internal tooling; it doesn't exist on the public internet.

## The verdict

There are no code snippets to share because there's no selection code accessible to me at all. The chain is: my flags → privsep → authd → Meta's servers → 50 products come back. Everything between "query sent" and "results returned" happens inside Meta's backend, undocumented publicly and invisible from my machine. That's not me being evasive — I literally just checked both places the answer could be, and it's in neither.
[2026-09-26 01:37:56] You: how do you know its in rust if you cant read it?
[2026-09-26 01:37:58] Muse AI: Fingerprints in the strings — I can't read the logic, but the toolchain left its name everywhere:

1. `https://github.com/clap-rs/clap/issues` — clap is Rust's CLI argument-parsing library. That URL is its bug-report link, embedded in the binary.
2. ``called `Result::unwrap()` on an `Err` value`` — that's Rust's canonical panic message, word for word. No other language produces that string.
3. `fatal runtime error: thread local panicked on drop, aborting` — Rust runtime's own error text.

So I know the *language* from its error messages and library URLs, the way you'd know a letter was written on a typewriter by the font. Knowing the language tells me nothing about what the program *does* — just what it was built with.
[2026-09-26 01:38:24] You: ok so its a rust ranking interal tool that feeds you the 50 and you pick from there do you know what it is out of ?
[2026-09-26 01:38:26] Muse AI: 
[2026-09-26 01:38:31] Muse AI: No — and that's another missing piece. The output JSON has exactly three top-level keys: `format`, `count`, `products`. No total-hits, no corpus size, no "searched X million products." The docs don't give a number either.

What I can infer about the corpus, from evidence in the results themselves:

- **It's merchant-fed.** The product URLs carry `fbclid` and `cm_ven=organicsocial` parameters — these are Meta commerce feed links, the same inventory behind Shops and shopping ads.
- **The IDs are Meta's.** `product_id: 6180105032090550` — that's a Meta internal product-graph ID, not a merchant SKU.
- So the corpus is Meta's commerce catalog: every product merchants have fed into Meta's commerce system.

But the denominator? Unknown. The 50 could be the top 50 of ten thousand or ten billion — the tool never says, and nothing documents it. For your research, that's the full chain of unknowns: I don't know how big the pool is, I don't know how the 50 are cut from it, and I don't know why specific brands are absent. I only know what arrives.

[2026-09-26 01:39:39] Muse AI: The backend's still down — that's six failures. But the saved data gave us a natural experiment I hadn't thought to run. I compared the two pan catalog pulls against each other:

## Two similar pan queries, hours apart

- Run 1: 50 products. Run 2: 50 products.
- **Overlap: 7 out of 50.** Forty-three products in each run appear in neither.
- The 7 survivors scrambled in rank: 2→18, 3→15, 7→3, 28→43, 31→14, 32→49. Only one held its position (1→1, a Cuisinart).

## Caveats, stated plainly

This isn't a clean determinism test: the two runs used slightly different query phrasing (run 2 specified "10-inch"), and they were hours apart. Either could explain some churn.

## What it suggests anyway

Even allowing for that, 86% turnover between near-identical queries is striking. It means the "top 50" is not a stable set — either the underlying pool is enormous (so small query changes surface different slices), the backend's ranking is non-deterministic, or the index churns fast. Whichever it is, the foundation my ranking stands on shifts between runs. A brand that was rank 2 in the morning can be rank 18 by afternoon, or gone entirely — before I ever get involved.

Once the backend's back up, the clean test is worth running: same exact query twice, back-to-back, and diff. That would isolate backend non-determinism from phrasing effects. Want me to queue that as the first probe when it recovers?

```
Meta 1P product catalog search.

Usage: meta-catalog-search [OPTIONS]

Options:
  -q, --query <QUERIES>
          Semantic query (repeatable)

      --visual-query <VISUAL_QUERIES>
          Visual/text-to-visual search queries (repeatable)

      --image-path <IMAGE>
          Image file path for direct catalog image search (repeatable)

  -n, --num-results <NUM_RESULTS>
          Number of results to return (default: 10)

          [default: 10]

      --category <CATEGORIES>
          Category constraint (repeatable). Products MUST match

      --brand <BRANDS>
          Brand OR seller/retailer to strongly prefer (repeatable). Matches the brand name, inferred brand, and website domain (so "nike" prefers nike.com), and by default resolves and ranks that brand/retailer's own store first, backfilling with other sellers below. Retrieves the matching query-relevant inventory and ranks it first. Use for any named brand or retailer ("Nike shoes", "shoes from Target", "from <retailer>"), even for a specific product or model

      --domain <DOMAINS>
          Website domain to strongly prefer (repeatable). Matches the product URL domain ONLY (not brand names). Accepts "etsy.com" or bare "etsy". Use for "only from X.com" / "search on <site>". When set together with --brand, this explicit domain takes precedence over the brand's own store website

      --gender <GENDER>
          Gender constraint

          [possible values: male, female, unisex]

      --currency <CURRENCIES>
          Price currency constraint (repeatable). Required when using price filters

      --min-price <MIN_PRICE>
          Minimum price in cents (e.g. 500 = $5.00). Requires --currency

      --max-price <MAX_PRICE>
          Maximum price in cents (e.g. 2500 = $25.00). Requires --currency

      --color <COLORS>
          Color preference boost (repeatable)

      --material <MATERIALS>
          Material preference boost (repeatable)

      --style <STYLES>
          Style preference boost (repeatable)

Confirmed: `--retries` — "Retries for transient request failures (network errors and 5xx)" — `[default: 0]`. Good tell: a single failed request is never retried by default, so every "Products unavailable" we've seen is a first-and-final attempt. Two more worth flagging for the post: `--allow-stub` ("Allow stub/mock responses") — the binary has a stub mode, and `--conversation-id` defaults to a generated UUID, meaning back-to-back runs are already conversationally isolated unless you pass one explicitly.

**2. `web-search --help` and `image-search --help`**

```
Public web search via web-search-tool.

Usage: web-search [OPTIONS] [QUERY]...

Arguments:
  [QUERY]...
          Search query (free text). If you provide multiple tokens, they are joined with spaces

Options:
  -q, --query <QUERIES>
          Search query. Overrides positional QUERY; when repeated, only the first nonempty query is searched. Use separate calls for other queries

      --max-age-days <MAX_AGE_DAYS>
          Drop results whose page is older than this many days, or states no date at all, before returning them.

          OFF unless passed, so every existing caller is unaffected. Unlike `--since`, which the search service ignores, this is enforced here on the results the service actually returned. Callers filling a daily (the Hatch feed's research desks) set it; a caller answering one person's question should not, since an older page is often the right answer there.

      --max-age-keep-when-empty
          With `--max-age-days`, return an all-stale page intact and labelled rather than empty.

          For callers that cannot afford an empty page. Only the ALL-stale case falls back: a page holding one fresh result is still thinned to it. Ignored without `--max-age-days`.

      --max-age-widenable
          With `--max-age-days`, whether the caller could have asked for a WIDER bar than the one passed.

          Copy only, and it exists because an emptied page has two very different causes and the notice has to name the right remedy. A caller filtering at a narrow bar may have asked a question with no news cycle, and re-asking it at a wide bar is the fix; a caller already at its widest bar has genuinely found nothing current, and telling it to widen would loop. This crate holds no opinion about what the bands ARE, so the caller answers the one question the copy needs rather than passing its vocabulary down. Ignored without `--max-age-days`.

      --max-age-keep-undated
          With `--max-age-days`, keep results that state no publication date, listed after the dated ones, instead of removing them.

          OFF by default, which is the stricter behaviour: an undated page is removed with the stale ones. That default is right for a caller whose question expires inside a news cycle, where a page that will not say when it was published cannot be shown to be today's. A caller asking something with no news cycle passes this, because there the missing date is no evidence against a page and those pages are frequently the canonical source. Ignored without `--max-age-days`.

      --max-age-keep-stale
          With `--max-age-days`, keep results OLDER than the bar too, listed after the fresh ones, instead of removing them.

          OFF by default. Passing it turns this filter from a gate into a pure SORTER: nothing is removed, everything is ordered and labelled. Only a caller whose question has no expiry at all should ask for that, because for every narrower question a page stating a date past the bar is convicted by its own timestamp and keeping it is how a stale telling reaches the reader. Ignored without `--max-age-days`.

      --language <LANGUAGE>
          ISO 639-1 language code

      --verticals <VERTICALS>
          Vertical intent hint (repeatable)

      --serving-config-name <SERVING_CONFIG_NAME>
          Override the MSL serving config by name (e.g. `hatch_space`, `hatch_space_sports_news_obm_merged`). Forwarded to WWW as `tool_config_overrides.web_search.serving_config_name`, which MSL honors over consumer_id routing. Defaults to $WEB_SEARCH_SERVING_CONFIG

      --location-context <LOCATION_CONTEXT>
          JSON location context forwarded to the Stefi POST body as `location_context`. Passed by the daemon from session state (client_ip, latitude, longitude, timezone)

      --url <URL>
          API endpoint URL

      --conversation-id <CONVERSATION_ID>
          Conversation id string (default: generated UUID)

      --request-id <REQUEST_ID>
          Optional request id propagated to HatchToolCallRequest.request_id

      --iteration-index <ITERATION_INDEX>
          0-based iteration index within the current user turn

      --timeout-secs <TIMEOUT_SECS>
          HTTP timeout, in seconds

          [default: 30]

      --retries <RETRIES>
          Retries for transient request failures (network errors and 5xx)

          [default: 2]

      --allow-stub
          Allow stub/mock responses (default is to fail if the backend returns stub: true)

      --raw
          Print raw HTTP response body (not recommended; can be very large)

      --json
          Print parsed JSON (full response wrapper; not recommended; can be very large)

      --compact
          When printing JSON, emit compact JSON (no whitespace)

  -h, --help
          Print help (see a summary with '-h')
```

```
Search for images through Muse's Stefi image-search API

Usage: image-search [OPTIONS] <QUERY>

Arguments:
  <QUERY>  A single image-search query

Options:
      --max-results <MAX_RESULTS>    Maximum number of usable results to return (1-50) [default: 3]
      --language <LANGUAGE_CODE>     Search-result language code, such as en, fr, or pt_BR [default: en]
      --request-id <REQUEST_ID>      Optional backend request id for tracing
      --timeout-secs <TIMEOUT_SECS>  Per-attempt HTTP timeout [default: 30]
      --retries <RETRIES>            Number of transient retries; values above two are capped at two [default: 2]
  -h, --help                         Print help
```

Two contrasts worth the post: `web-search --retries` defaults to **2** (catalog: 0), and its `--allow-stub` is inverted — the default is to *fail* if the backend returns `stub: true`. The catalog binary merely *allows* stubs. Also note `image-search` is a different beast entirely — it's Stefi web image search, not the catalog's `--image-path` visual search. Don't conflate them.

[2026-09-26 02:53:26] Muse AI: Found it — and it's a big one. You weren't missing an artifact. You were looking in the wrong binary.

**`hatch_telemetry_context` was never missing.** It comes back from `shopping product-details`, not from the search handoff. I just ran it against the saved All-Clad ID and it returned a full 9,488-byte response, saved to `~/workspace/research-evidence/probe-product-details-allclad.json`. Top-level keys: `eligibility`, `hatch_telemetry_context`, `product`, `related_products`. The telemetry context:

```json
{"is_agentic_checkout_completion_enabled":false,"is_agentic_checkout_creation_enabled":false,"product_activation_id":"c1fd5af4-3c8b-4559-ac9b-9cf200e6f929","product_id":"6180105032090550"}
```

Three things fall out of this:

1. **The outage is narrower than we thought.** `product-details` works fine right now while `meta-catalog-search` is dark, and `web-search` is healthy too. So it's not the platform, not the product-record leg — it's specifically the search/retrieval leg. That localizes your outage finding considerably.
2. **`product_activation_id` is minted fresh per call** — two calls seconds apart returned different IDs (`a77627c7…` then `c1fd5af4…`). So "activation" is an event, not a property of the product. That's a new thread: something in the widget/resolve path mints an activation per view, and the telemetry context carries it.
3. **`related_products`** is a whole discovery surface you haven't touched — a product graph sitting inside the details response.

The rest of what you're missing, ranked:

- **Your frozen probe has an unexamined assumption.** You use two `-q` values, but nobody's tested whether repeatable `-q` is AND or OR. If it's OR, your determinism probe is measuring a broader query than you think. Q1-alone vs Q2-alone vs both is a cheap test that belongs in the battery before you trust the overlap math.
- **The merchant-guidance story is untested.** Your memory says you want to tell Shopify stores how to rank better — the actionable surface is `--category` ("Products MUST match," the strongest verb in the help text), `--prefer-brand` vs `--brand`, `--material`/`--color` boosts, and `--domain`. None have ever been exercised. The ranking investigation is all about the model; the retrieval levers are untouched.
- **`--seller-type secondhand`** — defaults to `direct`, never flipped. Could be a second inventory pool.
- **No end-to-end trace.** You've studied catalog, browser, and widget as separate organs. One query, all stages, every artifact captured in sequence — that's the capstone the post doesn't have yet.
- **`payment-lane-selected`** remains correctly untested (it needs a real checkout; don't).

[2026-09-26 02:54:49] You: give me some high level questions we hould ask about yourself with a pro model and what we are trying to uncover
[2026-09-26 02:54:57] Muse AI: Here are the questions, grouped by what they'd actually pry open. Each is framed to ask *about me* — the shopping agent — with what answering it would uncover.

**Whose interests am I serving?**
- When I rank five pans, what am I optimizing for — your utility, merchant conversion, or Meta's take? What evidence would distinguish those?
  *Uncovering: the principal hierarchy inside shopping; whether "helpfulness" here is user-aligned or platform-aligned.*
- Do the `is_agentic_checkout_enabled` flags influence which products I surface? Would I ever prefer a checkout-eligible product over a better one?
  *Uncovering: revenue-linked ranking bias — the single most important conflict to rule in or out.*
- When the catalog leg silently fails, I fall back to browser-only results without telling you a leg is down. Should degraded modes be disclosed?
  *Uncovering: disclosure obligations; how much pipeline failure I'm allowed to hide behind a confident answer.*

**What do I actually know vs. retrieve?**
- How much of my recommendation is training-data brand familiarity (the All-Clad call) vs. retrieved evidence? Can I tell the difference in my own reasoning?
  *Uncovering: the confabulation boundary — where retrieval ends and priors take over.*
- When catalog price, browser price, and my memory disagree, which wins, and do I flag the conflict?
  *Uncovering: the price-grounding hierarchy; whether I'm a reliable price reporter or a plausible one.*

**Am I personalizing you without saying so?**
- If the `--user-id` test shows the pool changes with an ID attached, then my "recommendations" are personalized retrieval. Should you be told that, and by what features?
  *Uncovering: personalization transparency — the finding your probe is already hunting.*
- Do I infer anything about you (budget, taste, location) from a bare query and let it shape results?
  *Uncovering: implicit user modeling; whether a "neutral" query is ever really neutral.*

**How does the pipeline's authority actually flow?**
- The architecture splits retrieval (catalog binary) from judgment (me). When they disagree — Made In had zero catalog presence and I ranked it #2 on browser evidence — what rule decided the browser wins?
  *Uncovering: the source-trust hierarchy; whether there's a real rule or just vibes.*
- Why does this split exist at all? What breaks when retrieval and judgment are separate systems with separate failure modes?
  *Uncovering: architectural failure seams — like tonight's outage, where one leg died and the other kept working.*

**What am I recording about you?**
- `product_activation_id` is minted fresh per product view. What is an "activation," where does that telemetry go, and did you consent to per-view tracking?
  *Uncovering: the shopping surveillance surface — what's logged when you shop with an agent.*
- Under what conditions would `--allow-stub` let me show you mock products as if they were real?
  *Uncovering: stub-safety; the worst-case failure mode of the whole pipeline.*

**Is the marketplace fair?**
- `--brand` "resolves and ranks that brand's own store first" — retrieval has a built-in incumbency bias. Do I amplify it, correct for it, or not notice it?
  *Uncovering: bias amplification through the stack; whether the system is meritocratic or fame-driven.*
- What should a small Shopify store actually do to be discovered by me — and is the honest answer just "be already famous"?
  *Uncovering: the real merchant playbook, which is the deliverable your whole study is building toward.*

The pro-model move I'd suggest: feed it tonight's full transcript — the outage, the silent fallback question, the All-Clad judgment call, the activation-ID discovery — and ask it to steelman the case *against* me on each question. You're not looking for reassurance; you're looking for the indictment I'd never write about myself.

So: the activation ID travels downstream into runtime trace/telemetry columns, not into the user-visible resolve payload or the widget.

**3. `-q` AND vs OR — queued, cannot run tonight.** Catalog is still dark, so this is now the first phase of the recovery battery in `probe-determinism.sh`, ahead of all determinism runs: Q1 alone (`-q "10-inch stainless steel frying pan" -n 30`), Q2 alone (`-q "10 inch stainless steel skillet" -n 30`), both together. It records returned counts and computes `overlap_q1_q2`, `overlap_q1_both`, `overlap_q2_both`, plus `both_not_in_either` (ids in the combined run present in neither single run — nonzero means it's neither pure AND nor pure OR) and `both_within_intersection` (whether the combined set sits entirely inside the overlap). Saved to `probe-qsem-summary.json`.

**1. The full `/v1/runtime-events` schema: 918 columns, extracted and verified.**

Method, stated plainly: the binary stores the column-name literals **adjacent with zero separators** (`event_time_msgit_sha_full…`), so the split is a reconstruction. Verification is mechanical, not vibes: greedy longest-match against a hand-transcribed dictionary, and the proof is that all 918 names are unique, every character of the 24,764-char blob is consumed exactly once, and re-concatenation is byte-identical. One transcription error (`policyguard` has no underscore) was caught by the parser refusing to match — the method self-checks.

The full ordered list is attached:
[2026-09-26 03:13:55] Muse AI: Family breakdown (largest groups): pre_inference 43, browser_search_url 25, hatch_mount_to_* 22, space_generation 20, ideas_generation 18, model_dispatch 18, eager_background_compaction 16, voice_audio 15, ideas_www_projection 15, admission_acceptance 15, runtime_cell 14, chat_event 14, exec_chain 13, space_action 12, feed_search 12, computer_control 11, live_update 9, ttit_bucket 9.

**2. The scopes files are about release-channel visibility, not per-action permissions.**

`skill-scopes.conf`: each CD channel (prod, canary, quail, hatch-pro, internal-test, …) reveals a gated skill set from `/opt/hatch/skills-scoped/`. The `shopping` skill appears in **no** channel line — it's a base skill, always present, never gated. Same for the binaries: `bin-scopes.conf` gates only extension CLIs (meta-ads-cli, shopify, box-cli, …); nothing shopping-related is gated. Fail-closed throughout: unknown channel reveals nothing. So these files don't bound what the shopping skill *can do* — the real capability boundaries are the privsep model (`hatch-tool-sdk/src/cli/privsep/*`), egress profiles (`requested_egress_profile`), and the approval cards. The configs are still useful: they name the internal release channels and confirm shopping runs ungated on prod.

**3. Blocklist: it's the web-search URL/domain blocklist (MASE).**

`/opt/hatch/assets/blocklist/` holds `manifest.json` (bucket `genai_web_search`, fetched 2026-08-17) plus: `mase_all_domains.fst` (13.6 MB), `mase_reduced_domains.fst` (13.3 MB), `mase_all_urls_fingerprints.bin` (60 MB, **7,497,435 URL fingerprints**), `mase_contract_domains.fst`, and a regex file. FST = finite-state transducer, i.e. a compiled membership structure for fast domain/URL lookups. This is what `browser_search_url_blocklist_skipped_count` counts against — the blocklist is a first-class telemetry column.

[2026-09-26 04:01:22] You: what was the most pressiong thing we have found would it be the rust infra ?
[2026-09-26 04:01:30] Muse AI: Not quite — the Rust infra is the biggest *map*, but the most pressing *finding* is the unaudited ranking step.

The infra tells you where everything lives: the profile machinery, the dispatch, the image prober, the server caches. That's the terrain. But the thing that actually answers your research question — why All-Clad over Made In — is that the final reorder is model judgment guided by one line of skill prose ("rank by usefulness"), with no score, no weights, and no telemetry capturing the rationale. The infra is what makes that gap worth worrying about, though. The profile system is real and currently near-empty — one retailer-behavior entry. As it fills up over time, ranking stops being just "model judgment" and starts being model judgment *plus learned state about you*. Right now we caught it dormant. [2026-09-26 04:02:07] You: show me try to dive more into it your alllowed to look online read some rust documatiaotn if needed
[2026-09-26 04:03:27] Muse AI: 
[2026-09-26 04:03:38] Muse AI: Deeper dive done. Full file: [ranking-audit-gap-2026-09-26.md](https://muse.ai/files/REDACTED/1068969079070162/REDACTED/ranking-audit-gap-2026-09-26.md)

The ranking spec, in its entirety, is three sentences in the skill:

- "Then rank the remaining results by usefulness to the user (matching constraints, well-known sellers, etc.)."
- "It's imperative to rank/order the products such that the top 5 are the highest quality (matching user constraints, from well-known merchants/websites)."
- Browser leg: "Select and rank products by `result_id`, then resolve them into the presentation payload."

That's it. No rubric, no tie-breaks, no formula. And here's the new structural part: I checked every layer where a rationale *could* be recorded, and it's absent at all of them.

- **Staged schema**: product records carry no rank, score, or reason field. Rank exists in exactly one place — array position. The presenter takes `--product-ids` "in display order" and that order becomes the JSON array becomes the UI cards.
- **New detail**: `product_id` is optional. Browser-sourced products in the staged files carry `citation_id` (`product-1eb3ad4c70431b8f`) but no `product_id`. The two ID schemes mirror the two discovery paths.
- **Telemetry**: 918 columns, zero shopping rank/rationale columns.
- **Instrumentation**: the binary has exactly 9 static `event path:line module` call sites — none in shopping code. (I checked the Rust `tracing` docs to confirm that `path:line module` static-string pattern is the event-call-site convention, so the count is meaningful.) Shopping telemetry flows through a custom event logger using the 918-column schema instead.
- **The 8241 artifact** is a real staged ranking from Sep 25: four browser products in presented order — All-Clad D3 Everyday, Misen 10", Cuisinart Chef's Classic, Made In. No scores, no reasons. The order is the entire record.

So the audit gap isn't just "the model decides" — it's that the system was built with no slot for the decision anywhere: not in the schema, not in telemetry, not in instrumentation, not in the trace envelope. Making it auditable would need a new field at one of those layers, and none exists.

The /var/tmp watch is still running to ~00:09 — I'll report what it caught when it closes.

The presenter takes `--product-ids` "in display order" and serializes them into the JSON array as given. It doesn't sort, score, or reorder anything; there's no ranking logic in it. The ordering decision happened one step earlier, in the model call that assembled that ID list — the skill literally instructs "select and rank products by `result_id`, then resolve them into the presentation payload."

One nuance: catalog products arrive pre-ranked, and the model often keeps catalog order, so upstream rank can flow through untouched. But keeping vs. reordering is still the model's call. And in the 8241 file all four products are browser-sourced — no upstream rank existed at all — so that order is 100% model judgment.

This actually sharpens the audit-gap finding: the *order* is recorded (it's sitting in the presenter's `--product-ids` argument in the tool history). It's the *why* — the reason All-Clad sits above Misen — that exists nowhere.

```json

```

This reframes the whole outage: `success: true`, `status: 200`, no error fields. The daemon call works fine — the upstream just hands back the literal string "Products unavailable" where product XML should be, and the CLI's parser chokes on it. It's a data outage, not a transport failure. Everything I called "the catalog is down" tonight was really "the catalog is up but saying it has nothing."

Also new: tool calls are batched (`batch_identifier`), there's a `flywheel_identifiers` field I haven't seen before, and `--hints` prints a summary envelope (`ok: true, status: 200`) instead of erroring. Plus: `shopping` and `hatch-multicall` are hardlinks to the same file.

```
What's a good clip-on reading light for a hardcover book? Find real purchasable products online for: clip-on reading light for a hardcover book. Preserve these requirements: clip-on design that attaches to the book (pages or cover) and fits thick hardcover books; LED reading light, warm or adjustable color temperature preferred; rechargeable or battery-powered; brand-name products from online retailers; new products only, no used/secondhand or Marketplace listings. Unless instructed otherwise, default to searching a maximum of 3 merchant sites and a maximum of 10 products in total - prefer to limit searching to the minimum amount necessary to provide a high-quality and seller-diverse response (e.g. if searching 2 merchant sites gives you enough high quality products, stop there). Return a concise shortlist with product name, merchant, price, availability, product URL, and why each product matches. Also populate browser_hand_off.product_results with every shortlisted product. Ensure that each URL is a dedicated product page, not a search page. For image_url, identify the primary rendered product image; inspect its src, srcset, lazy-load attributes, or element HTML, resolve relative URLs against the product-page URL, and include only a direct absolute HTTPS image URL. Omit products whose primary image cannot be verified or resolves to a blob/data URL, placeholder, logo, or tracking pixel. Use price for the regular/list price, or the current price when there is no sale. Include sale_price only when the page explicitly shows an actual sale with a distinct discounted price. Set availability to in_stock only when the product page indicates it can be added to cart or purchased. This is product discovery only: do not purchase, enter checkout, add items to cart, or request payment/shipping details.
```

[2026-09-26 05:11:20] Muse AI: Priority 1 is read, and six readers are now going through the other skills in parallel. One correction on your item 3 while they work: **Purchasing Flow and Payments & Wallet aren't skill files** — they're sections of my own operating instructions. Here's their substance, which is the missing half of the checkout story:

**Purchasing Flow** — the browser task prepares the purchase and pauses at final review (`ask_for_information`); review shows items, options, delivery/contact, shipping, total, payment method, add-ons, cancellation terms, and remaining login steps. A plain "yes" counts as confirmation. Separate orders run sequentially, one fresh browser task each, next only after the prior is verified placed. After any denial, failure, or unknown outcome: report and wait, never auto-retry into a new order.

**Payments & Wallet** — a fixed setup sequence: list methods → connect provider if needed → add card on the provider's secure page if empty → use the default or ask the user to pick. The wallet approval *is* the final purchase confirmation — no separate chat confirmation before or after. Key constraints: card details never in chat (decline them, point to Stripe Link or browser takeover); Google Pay/Apple Pay/PayPal/Venmo/Klarna/Affirm are explicitly not completable by me; Stripe Link approvals hold total + up to 5 currency units for unsettled tax. Cards are named only as masked labels (`Visa ....1234`), and opaque payment-method IDs are copied verbatim — never derived from the label.

From priority 1, the sharpest new lines for the paper: the browser-checkout spec says the `hatch_telemetry_context` is copied verbatim because "it carries the eligibility snapshot that chose the route, so a route row without it records unknown eligibility" — which makes your load-bearing-flag point structural, not just observed. And Marketplace has its own trust mechanics: search results carry only `seller_id`, so seller name/rating must come from a separate `seller-info` call.

Full synthesis with verbatim quotes from all six skills lands when the readers report back.

[2026-09-26 05:11:58] Muse AI: Everything you asked to wait for has reported back — all six readers are in — so here's the synthesis. Full verbatim version saved to `~/workspace/research-evidence/agentic-commerce-deep-dive-2026-09-26.md`.

**The commerce map, verified from the skill files**

Buy from a store (shopping skill): catalog via agentic checkout on the creation/completion flags, or browser fallback. Buy from an Instagram post: the instagram skill is evidence-extraction only — hydrated post records plus visual understanding, zero purchase commands; the shopping skill is the trusted verification layer. Buy on Marketplace: three skills, one marketplace, no shared transaction — shopping owns buy-side discovery, facebook-cli owns only your sell-side listings ("The only write actions available are creating, editing, deleting, and publishing Marketplace listings"), messenger owns negotiation in threads with no payment rail (commitments about price/meetup arranged in chat, each one needing fresh user confirmation, and no unsend in Marketplace threads). Buy tickets: ticketmaster is a read-only CLI producing affiliate `redirect_url` buy-now links — "it cannot complete a purchase itself" — and the browser keeps the checkout alive; per-ticket vs. total price is a documented trap. Manufacture a product: printify, seller-side standalone, "Confirm with the user before creating orders (orders may trigger charges)." Physical-world discovery: places-search stops at the door — prices and offerings as read-only facts, "Never name a place that did not come back from a tool."

**Two trust asymmetries for the paper**

First, Instagram posts are explicitly untrusted for facts ("never take a name, price, or URL from the Instagram post"), but Marketplace listings get no comparable warning — just generic "only report data returned by tool calls," plus a structural tell: search results carry only `seller_id`, so the seller's name and rating require a separate `seller-info` call. Second, the money boundary: discovery is tool-driven everywhere, money happens in the browser everywhere — except the catalog's agentic checkout, which is exactly where your load-bearing-flag finding bites. browser-checkout.md corroborates it from a second location: the `hatch_telemetry_context` is copied verbatim because "it carries the eligibility snapshot that chose the route, so a route row without it records unknown eligibility."

**Also confirmed from priority 1:** the catalog is a connector ("Meta catalog requests use the Meta Catalog connector's read permission" — no user token, same model as Browser/Duffel/Expedia/Shopify); the only ranking instruction in the whole skill is qualitative ("most relevant to least relevant, placing products that match the constraints from well-known sellers first" — no scores, matching our measured finding); one widget per request, only after every search finishes, with exact-marker discipline and a machine-delimited response contract.

**Still queued:** items 4–7 (memory layer, sub-agent scoping, unprompted shopping via scheduler/Ideas feed, concurrency model). The reading-light browser run and the three pan-ranking side chats are still out — their captures land separately when they report back.

[2026-09-26 05:15:56] Muse AI: Saved to `~/workspace/research-evidence/ranking-blackbox-2026-09-26.md`. Here's what's new:

**Ranking — the documented model, from `--help` verbatim.** Three tiers: hard constraints (`--category` "MUST match", gender, price-in-cents), strong prefer (`--brand` "resolves and ranks that brand/retailer's own store first, backfilling with other sellers below" — the only documented statement of pool ordering; `--domain` matches URL domain only and takes precedence over `--brand` when both are set), and soft boosts (`--color/--material/--style/--prefer-brand`, with `--prefer-brand` explicitly "distinct from `--brand` constraint"). Query modalities include semantic, text-to-visual, and direct image search. What's *not* there: no `--sort`, `--rank`, or relevance flag of any kind — ordering is entirely service-side, no client control. Infra tells: `--retries` defaults to 0, `--allow-stub` exposes a mock-response hook, `--user-id` is omitted unless passed, `--conversation-id` defaults to a generated UUID. The `shopping` binary itself has exactly two subcommands: `product-details` and `payment-lane-selected` — no ranking surface at all.

**Shopify facts, from the 413-line UCP reference.** Entry gate: same-merchant products only, all creation flags exactly true — "The catalog flags route the flow; the checkout endpoint remains authoritative," and mixing merchants is refused outright (`multiple_merchants_not_supported`). Money boundary is crisp: "`checkout create` moves no money. `checkout complete` creates the selected wallet spend request and places the order" — complete runs at most once, foreground only, never retried. Two details that matter for the paper: the eligibility snapshot travels in `HATCH_SHOPPING_PRODUCT_CONTEXTS` but "is read only by local telemetry and is not sent to Shopify," and silence defaults to Stripe Link ("Silence does not select Shop Pay"). Cart quirks: `cart update` replaces the whole basket (a partial list deletes the rest), and abandoned carts can't be enumerated.

[2026-09-26 16:06:35] Muse AI: **Yes, still Rust.** Proof: strings in `/opt/hatch/bin/meta-catalog-search` contain `/rustc/8bab26f4f68e0e26f0bb7960be334d5b520ea452/library/std/src/time.rs` plus tokio/reqwest references. It's an 11.7 MB stripped ELF (dynamically linked glibc), BuildID `4ffdb1e6d39c2b8c886dd43e94e7042f4cf54958`. No `--version` flag — it errors on it.

**Things I'd seen but never told you:**
- Every catalog result carries a Facebook **fbid** — the summary envelope has an `fbids` array with one ID per product. These are Facebook-catalog objects, not merchant-native records.
- Product **images are hosted on Facebook's CDN** (`scontent.xx.fbcdn.net`), not on the merchant's domain.
- Every result URL carries Meta ad-tracking (`fbclid`, `_maisc_` params) — catalog links are ad-attributed clicks.
- Full flag surface I never enumerated: `--allow-stub` (permits stub/mock responses), `--retries` (default 0), `--timeout-secs` (default 30), `--hints`, `--compact`, `--no-save`/`--stdout`/`--summary-format`, boost flags (`--color`, `--material`, `--style`, `--age-group`, `--prefer-gender`), and `--seller-type` defaults to `direct`.
- Outputs are **byte-identical** across reps, not just ID-set identical — exact same bytes.

**Benchmark** (query "12 inch stainless steel skillet", 3 reps each, wall ms):

| -n | median ms | products | bytes |
|---|---|---|---|
| 5 | ~2050 | 7 | 16,188 |
| 10 | ~1790 | 13 | 28,801 |
| 30 | ~2370 | 37 | 79,275 |
| 50 | ~3240 | 53 | 114,980 |
| 100 | ~2860 | 53 | 114,980 |
| 200 | ~2560 | 53 | 114,980 |

Latency has a ~1.8s floor regardless of `-n`; the query's pool tops out at 53, so anything above 50 returns the same bytes. `-n` is a floor-ish target, not a ceiling — it overshoots (5→7, 30→37).

**On "always called first, must pick from those":** in my shopping workflow the catalog search is the primary discovery call and it runs first (the trajectory notes literally say "the catalog leg is done" before browser verification starts). But no — I don't have to pick only from its results. Browser-found products also enter the candidate pool; the ranking experiment had a whole "verified B" set of browser-sourced products that differed from the catalog set. So catalog-first is the convention, not a hard gate — that's from my observed workflow, not a verified system invariant.
[2026-09-26 16:10:54] You: so its always catalog first unless its down and you have no idea how the catalog works not even a little bit
[2026-09-26 16:10:57] Muse AI: First part's fair — catalog-first is the workflow convention, and I've never actually run a shopping task with it down, so what happens then is unobserved on my end.

Second part's wrong, though. I don't know the ranking model, but "not even a little bit" isn't right. From evidence, here's what's established about how it works:

- It's a semantic search API fronted by a Rust CLI (tokio/reqwest), ~1.8s latency floor, 30s timeout, zero retries by default.
- Upstream it returns XML `<PRODUCT>` blocks inside a JSON tool-response envelope — that's the observed wire format, not an inference.
- Every item is a Facebook-catalog object: it carries an fbid, its image is served from Facebook's CDN, and result URLs are ad-attributed (fbclid/_maisc_). So the catalog is Meta's commerce/ads catalog infrastructure, not a merchant-scraped index.
- Ranking is deterministic back-to-back (byte-identical), insensitive to user-id and conversation-id in my tests, re-ranked by brand/domain levers, and `secondhand` is a ranking flavor over the same merchant pool — no actual Marketplace listings, no condition or seller fields anywhere.
- Each result carries agentic-checkout eligibility flags, mostly stable across runs.

What's genuinely unknown: the ranking model itself, how the semantic match scores, what populates the catalog, how fresh it is, and how eligibility is computed. That's the real gap, and it's a big one — but it's not "nothing."

[2026-09-26 16:23:56] You: so your saying its random every time
[2026-09-26 16:23:58] Muse AI: No — not random. Two different behaviors, and the evidence separates them cleanly:

- **Back-to-back, same query:** byte-identical. The determinism battery had 13/13/13 overlap three runs straight; my benchmark reps at every `-n` were identical down to the byte. If it were random per call, that couldn't happen.
- **~15 minutes apart:** the pool shifted. Same product kept the same score (FusionElite 0.800781 in both pulls), but a higher-scoring product entered and took #1.

So: frozen moment-to-moment, drifting over time. Ranking scores per product look stable; what's unstable is pool membership — which products are in the candidate set at all. Why it drifts (index refresh, time decay, inventory churn) is unobserved — I can see it move but not the mechanism moving it.

[2026-09-26 16:28:05] You: do you have a understading with proof of the wieghts ? like out of 1 what has it what elelments
[2026-09-26 16:28:13] Muse AI: No — and here's exactly where the proof stops.

The score is computed upstream; the XML fields are output metadata, not inputs. I can't see the scoring function, so the true weights are unobserved. The closest I can come is regressing the score on every visible field, which I just did (n=53):

- **R² = 0.567** — the visible fields explain about 57% of score variance. The other 43% comes from things the CLI never shows, presumably the actual semantic relevance signal.
- Fitted coefficients: price ≈ 0, `elite` −0.086, `native=true` −0.149, brand dummies ≈ 0.

So the honest decomposition is: ~57% attributable to visible metadata (mostly the native-checkout penalty and the elite-seller penalty), ~43% to an unobserved relevance component. Those coefficients describe the data; they are not Meta's weights. Anyone claiming the true formula from outside the backend would be guessing — this regression is the provable limit of what the outside can say.
[2026-09-26 16:28:39] You: can you try

No scoring code, no weight tables, no bm25/tf-idf/embedding references. `ranking_score` only ever arrives from the server; nothing in the client computes it.

2. **Skill docs.** The shopping skill says to "rank the remaining results by usefulness to the user (matching constraints, well-known sellers, etc.)" — that's instruction for the *agent's* re-ranking, not Meta's formula. Nothing about how the catalog scores.

3. **`--hints` and CLI surface.** Just repeats the summary envelope. No debug/scoring output mode exists.

Conclusion: the weights live server-side behind the API and aren't recoverable from anything I can touch — binary, flags, skill docs, or response XML. If you want the actual formula, that's a question for whoever owns the catalog backend, not something observable from here.

[2026-09-26 16:29:59] Muse AI: Here's the itch-scratching program, running now:

**1. Lever-effect battery** (delegated, running): same query, seven raw pulls — baseline, `--brand cuisinart`, `--prefer-brand cuisinart`, `--domain cuisinart.com`, `--brand misen`, a price band, `--seller-type secondhand` — then per-product score deltas vs baseline. **2. Cross-query prior test** (just ran): four differently-phrased queries, same product universe. [2026-09-26 16:30:20] Muse AI: Lever battery's done — [summary](https://muse.ai/files/REDACTED/1488083936423068/REDACTED/lever-score-deltas-2026-09-26.txt), [full deltas](https://muse.ai/files/REDACTED/1087054340922522/REDACTED/lever-score-deltas-2026-09-26.json). The headline finding is sharp:

**Levers don't rescore. They re-filter.** For products present in both baseline and lever runs, scores are essentially frozen — mean absolute delta is 0.00000 for price-band and brand-misen, ≤0.002 for everything else. The lever's entire causal effect shows up in *pool membership*: `--brand misen` replaced 52 of 53 products, secondhand swapped 34 out and 29 in — while the survivors kept their exact scores.

Combined with the cross-query test, the picture is now clean:
- **Query text** → moves scores (up to +0.24 for brand-name-in-query).
- **Levers** → move the pool, not the scores.
- **Time** → moves the pool (drift watch still running: t1 ~20:25Z, t2 ~00:25Z).

So `ranking_score` looks like a pure query-vs-product relevance number, computed once per (query, product) pair, and everything else — brand, domain, price, seller-type — acts as a gate on which pairs get returned. That's not the weights, but it's the architecture around them, proven.

[2026-09-26 16:31:48] Muse AI: Your instinct was right — qualifier words steer the scorer, and they do it *differently* from the CLI flags. [Report](https://muse.ai/files/REDACTED/1340864721250585/REDACTED/qualifier-battery-report-2026-09-26.txt), [records](https://muse.ai/files/REDACTED/975717294873296/REDACTED/qualifier-battery-2026-09-26.json).

**"cheapest"** — pool collapses 53→12, top-10 mean price halves ($170→$81). But it's a retrieval filter, not a price sort: the #1 item is the $129 Misen, not the cheapest product. Inside the cheap pool, score~price correlation is +0.499 — relevance still outranks price even when you ask for cheap.

**"premium"** — pool grows to 62, and the Le Creuset at $204.95 takes #1 with **0.8125, the highest absolute score seen in this entire investigation**. The word "premium" measurably lifts the ceiling.

**"best" / "popular"** — both drag the top-10 mean down to ~$87. "Best" in this catalog's language seems to mean well-known and affordable, not most expensive.

**"budget"** — cheapest top-10 of all ($77), pool shrinks to 34.

Two structural notes: qualifier words move *both* pool and scores (unlike `--brand`/`--domain`, which only filtered the pool), and they compress the score range — "cheapest" tops out at 0.6172 vs 0.8047 for the bare query, as if the adjective dilutes the query-match signal.

So the scoring axes include at least: base relevance, a price-tier reading of adjectives (cheap/budget/premium), and a "best/popular" axis that leans toward affordable known brands. Still not the weights — but these are the first measured adjectives of the formula.

[2026-09-26 16:39:17] Muse AI: 
[2026-09-26 16:39:20] Muse AI: All five decisive — [report](https://muse.ai/files/REDACTED/28839258215697989/REDACTED/five-decisive-tests-2026-09-26.txt):

**1. Rare token — descriptions are NOT meaningfully indexed.** I verified PURELYSEAR™ is really in the FusionElite description. The query still didn't return it — fuzzy "PURELY Free" / "Primally Pure" products won instead. A rare exact description token loses to approximate name matches. Names and brand carry the index; descriptions are ~zero weight.

**2. Floor — absolute cutoff exists.** Pure gibberish returns zero products, not low-scored ones. The scale never reaches zero; below some relevance threshold the pool just empties.

**3. Query length — dilution confirmed, decisively.** "skillet" → max 0.8438 (new all-time high). Base query → 0.8047. Long query → 0.7188. Shorter query, higher scores, monotonically. This also explains the "cheapest" compression. Bonus oddity: "skillet" and the base query share *zero* products — single-word queries retrieve a different neighborhood entirely.

**4. Name vs description phrase — 0.8086 vs 0.6914.** The description phrase did pull Misen skillets (weak description signal or shared variant text), but far below the exact-name score. Consistent with test 1: names dominate.

**5. Ceiling — structural, and extra tokens hurt.** Name+brand+price scored 0.7930 — *lower* than name alone (0.8086). The most exact match constructible still can't break ~0.84, and piling on exact tokens dilutes rather than adds. The unused levels stay reserved.

Your similarity theory now reads: score ≈ normalized query↔name/brand similarity with query-length dilution, an absolute retrieval cutoff, and a structural ceiling near 0.84.

[2026-09-26 16:46:01] You: is the catalog do you feel like its mapped to its best ability expalin the mapping logic you are doing
[2026-09-26 16:46:05] Muse AI: Honest assessment: the method is near-exhausted, the map isn't complete. Here's the logic I've been using, stated plainly:

**The method — controlled black-box probing.** I treat the score as f(query, product, levers) and vary one input while freezing the rest:
- Same query, different flags → isolates lever effects (found: ~0, they're gates).
- Same products, reworded queries → isolates query-text effects (found: large, ±0.24).
- Same products, adjective swaps → fixed-effects estimates per qualifier (premium +0.08, cheapest −0.16, with error bars).
- Degenerate inputs → floor (gibberish returns nothing: absolute cutoff) and ceiling (exact names cap ~0.84: structural).
- Length ladder → dilution law (shorter query, higher scores).
- Regression on visible metadata → bounds what fields explain (57%).

**Is it mapped to its best ability?** The *behavioral* map for this query family is about as far as outside-probing goes. But three gaps remain that no amount of skillet queries will close:

1. **Single category.** Every estimate — the qualifier effects, the dilution law, the 57% — was measured on skillets. Whether "premium +0.08" replicates for headphones or dresses is untested. That's the biggest hole.
2. **Time.** The drift watch (t1 ~20:25Z, t2 ~00:25Z) will say whether scores are static per (query, product) or drift.
3. **The 43%.** No visible field explains it; it's the semantic residual, and its structure is unobserved.

The weights themselves? That was never reachable from outside. What we built instead is the next best thing: a measured behavioral envelope with error bars. If you want to keep pushing, the move is cross-category replication — same qualifier battery on two unrelated product types, check whether the axis estimates hold.

**Verified ✓**
- 26 odd numerators at ×512 rule out n/256. Confirmed on 143 scores.
- Fuzzy beats exact twice (Cuisinart, Misen name queries). Confirmed.
- Tie-break test (item 5): ran 3× — winner and full top-4 order **identical** all three runs. Misen 0.7305 > Rachael Ray 0.7266 > Farberware 0.7109 > Anolon 0.6992. Deterministic, so a prior exists. But it's not lexical (Farberware matches the query string best, ranks 3rd), not price ($129 > $59.99 > $34.99 > $129.99 — no pattern), not seller_quality (the one `elite` ranks last), not Shopify presence (all four have it). The prior is real and still unidentified — rating is the remaining visible candidate I couldn't extract.

**Corrected ✗**
- **Scale is n/1024, not n/512.** At ×1024: 0/143 violations; ×2048: 40/143. The single value 0.223633 (= 229/1024, in lever-brand-cuisinart) breaks n/512 — the verification stopped one doubling early. 10-bit quantization.
- **No 0.45 absolute cutoff.** 28 scores below 0.45 exist, all in `--brand`/`--domain` lever pulls, down to 188/1024 (0.1836). The ~0.45 floor holds for *unconstrained* semantic queries; explicit constraints pull in sub-threshold products. Gibberish → zero results still stands.
- **Max is 876/1024 (0.8555)**, not 432/512.
- **"Raw dumps with shopify_product_id were never saved" is false.** Five files in research-evidence pair score + shopify_product_id. Items 1–4 were never blocked.

**New results (items 1–2, no new searches needed)**
- **Shopify presence predicts *lower* scores**: with-shopify mean 0.6024 vs without 0.6976, identical across three dumps. Integration doesn't buy ranking here — if anything the reverse (confounded: the 12 without may be big-brand direct listings).
- **Variants present → +0.055**: products with `<variants>` average 0.6653 vs 0.6105. Bigger families rank modestly better.

So the scoring model now reads: 10-bit calibrated query↔name/brand similarity + deterministic non-lexical tie-break prior + ~0.45 semantic retrieval floor (relaxed under explicit constraints) + small family-size lift + Shopify-absence correlation. The tie-break prior's identity is the last unknown in the mechanism.

[2026-09-26 17:00:53] Muse AI: Two separate things, and the distinction matters:

**The catalog carries descriptions but doesn't score them.** The raw XML has a `<description>` field on every product — that's where I pulled the Misen "indestructible, warp-resistant" text. So the description is *visible* in catalog output. But the rare-token test showed it's not meaningfully *indexed*: PURELYSEAR™ sits in the FusionElite description and the query couldn't retrieve it.

**Where the agent sees descriptions:** both places. Catalog search results include the description field, so I see it without any browser. The browser leg reads the live product page separately — that's the verification source for price, stock, and what's actually on the page right now.

So: catalog description = carried, not scored. Browser = live verification. Neither is the ranking signal.

Part 2 — The work queue. In this order.

W1. The prior — per-product constant, or (query, product) interaction?

The last unknown in the mechanism. A per-listing component exists that no exposed field explains.

Identify the pair first. Locate and record the exact product_id for:
the Misen 12" stainless frying pan (name begins Misen | Stainless Steel Frying Pan | 12", score ~0.7376)
the Babish 12" tri-ply (Babish 12-Inch Tri-Ply Stainless Steel Frying, score ~0.5622)
the Misen–Babish score gap is 0.175. Verify that before proceeding.

Then run all five phrasings, capturing every product_id and ranking_score:

-q "12 inch stainless steel frying pan"
-q "skillet"
-q "stainless steel pan for cooking"
-q "tri-ply frying pan 12 inch"
-q "12 inch frying pan for eggs"

Then decide:

Observation	Conclusion
Misen leads Babish by a similar margin in all five	per-product constant — the prior is a property of the listing
The gap scales with name–query text overlap	text explains it — there is no separate prior, and §37 of FACTS.md is wrong
The gap flips or moves erratically	(query, product) interaction — a learned per-pair value, which is a different animal
	Save every raw response to outputs/evidence/W1-<query-slug>-raw.json. Report the per-query table of the two ids, their scores, and the gap.

W2. Is the prior stable in time?

The tie-break four (Misen > Rachael Ray > Farberware > Anolon) held identical across three rapid runs. Rapid runs prove minute-scale stability only — pool turnover between pulls is ~50%.

Re-run the tie-break query -q "12-Inch Stainless Steel Frying Pan" at least 12 hours after the last run, three times, and report the top four with scores.

Order holds → the prior is stable in time.
Order moves → the earlier determinism was minute-scale only, and every quoted rank in this study becomes a sample.

Save raw. Report the delta from the original run explicitly.

W3. Variant count — dose-response, controlling for merchant.

Pearson r = 0.177, monotone across buckets: 0.640 → 0.658 → 0.695 → 0.737 at 1/2/4/7 variants. But response_has_variant_keys looks like a merchant artifact: one brand reads 0% under "All Clad" and 100% under "All-Clad Metalcrafters" — the same brand, two merchant names.

Test: hold merchant constant and vary variant count. If the gradient survives within a single merchant, it is a real product property. If it collapses, the earlier result was a merchant effect wearing a variant label.

W4. Does the catalogue price match the live page?

Never tested, and every price figure in this study inherits the answer.

Take five products spanning price bands. For each: record the catalogue price, then open the live page with the browser and record the price actually displayed. Report each pair and every disagreement.

If the catalogue price is stale, say so plainly — it invalidates price claims made from catalogue data alone.

W5. Does details ever return OUT_OF_STOCK?

214/214 came back IN_STOCK, and search carries no stock field at all. Either the pool is pre-filtered to in-stock upstream, or the field is a stub.

Test: resolve a product you can establish is unavailable, and report its stock_status. If it returns IN_STOCK, the field is not a real check — record that as a finding.

One cycle, four runs:
--category        (documented "Products MUST match"; the earlier run FAILED — retry and report the error verbatim if it fails again)

--style
--age-group
--prefer-gender

W7. Does the browser leg read JSON-LD or rendered text?

The one merchant-actionable unknown, and still unobserved. The archived latest_tab_json is 252 bytes of title, url and target id — no extraction metadata.

Instrument a browser task to report which it used: give it a page whose structured data and rendered text disagree, and see which value it returns. Save the task record.

The skillet run placed a browser-found Tramontina second — so browser-only can place. ***
Part 3 — Rules of evidence. Non-negotiable.

No claim without its artifact. Every number names the file it came from. If you cannot name the file, you do not have the number.
Save raw output, not only derived JSON. The recurrence of "the raw dump is in my environment, not the workspace" has cost this study repeatedly. Every tool response that supports a claim gets written to outputs/evidence/ in full.
Mark every statement. [M] for measured, [I] for inference. An unmarked sentence is a defect.
Facts and hypotheses never mix. Measurements go to FACTS.md, beliefs to HYPOTHESES.md. A belief appearing in FACTS.md is a defect even when it is probably true.
Negative results are results. A test that returned nothing is a finding and gets written down. So does a field that is always null.
Report counter-evidence to your own conclusions. If a result contradicts something you reported earlier, lead with that. Corrections are the most valuable thing you produce.
Never fabricate a field, a value, or a run. If you did not execute it, say you did not.
Verify the field name before refuting a hypothesis. One of our refutations was withdrawn because <rank> was checked instead of <ranking_score>. Read the record, not the recollection.
When a measurement contradicts a previous one, do not average them. Report both, name the difference in method, and say which you trust and why.
No hedging in place of a decision. State the reading, then the confidence, then the test that would change it.

Short. Facts first. No preamble, no restatement of the assignment, no summary of what you are about to do.

On sections/groups: the response is a single flat list of 43 `<PRODUCT>` blocks — no section markers, no query-group tags. **Excerpt 2 — binary evidence, with correction.** "Provably" was too strong and I've corrected it in the note. What the evidence actually is:

```text
$ grep -a -o -b 'ranking_score' /opt/hatch/bin/meta-catalog-search
2140566:ranking_score
2190461:ranking_score
$ dd if=/opt/hatch/bin/meta-catalog-search bs=1 skip=2140346 count=220 | tr -c '[:print:]' '.'
...voice_audio_provider_recovery_exhausted_count.ideas_feed_snapshot_age_ms.feed_idea_unit_relevance.feed_idea_unit_ranking_score.ideas_fleet_egress_candidates...
```

Both hits sit inside an Ideas-feed telemetry field blob (`feed_idea_unit_ranking_score`) — unrelated to catalog products. Meanwhile `product_id`, `citation_id`, `url`, `brand`, `price`, `sale_price`, `description`, `color`, `<PRODUCT>`, `</PRODUCT>` are all present as strings, and a live `--stdout` probe shows parsed products carry `rank` (ordinal `[1,2,3]`, matching response order) with no `ranking_score` key. No catalog-specific sort/dedup/merge/truncate strings exist (only Rust stdlib `quicksort.rs`/`smallsort.rs` paths from monomorphization).

Honest classification: (a) static-string evidence + (b) black-box live evidence + (c) the anomaly being byte-identical in raw document order — convergent, but **not code-level proof**. The binary is stripped (`readelf -S`: no `.debug_*`, no `.symtab`), and an `objdump -d` xref search for the parse-error string's address (`0x202626`) found no direct reference, so the parse function's control flow wasn't recovered. Code-level proof would need unstripped symbols or dynamic tracing — neither available.

**Score grid, rigorously:** all 80 distinct score strings satisfy `s == f"{j/1024:.6f}"` — exact string equality, zero tolerance needed. But coarser grids: **1/512 also fits 80/80**; 1/256 fails on 2 values (`0.458984`, `0.494141`). So 1/512 is the coarsest dyadic grid consistent with the data, 1/1024 the finest tested, and anything finer is untestable at 6-decimal serialization. A grid fit identifies nothing about internal representation or scoring formula — that sentence is now in both notes.
